Mean-Reversion Prompt Research

A manager-facing summary of the model-epoch change, the current evidence, and what the historical prompt experiments mean.

Generated 2026-08-23 18:38:48 from local prompt constants and completed result artifacts. No LLM calls or backtests are run by this dashboard.

Bottom Line

A backend model change occurred July 31.

The strong 16%+ minimal-change results belong to the prior V4-Flash Preview epoch.

Post-switch reruns were weaker and more variable. Current research must be rebased on V4-Flash-0731.

Manager Summary

The July 31 Model Change Reset The Baseline

The prior model epoch performed better in the like-for-like rerun: the same anonymous minimal-change prompt at temperature 1.0 produced 16.77% CAGR and 1.400 Sharpe on V4-Flash Preview, versus 13.55% CAGR and 1.147 Sharpe on V4-Flash-0731—a 3.22 percentage-point CAGR drop and 0.253 Sharpe drop. This is a model-backend break, so results from the two epochs are historical references—not pooled or directly ranked experiments.
Model epochRun timingPrompt / settingsCAGRSharpeInterpretation
V4-Flash Preview (historical)Before July 31, 2026Minimal change · temp 1.0 · anonymous16.77%1.400Strong historical result; frozen reference only
V4-Flash-0731 (current)July 31, 2026 or laterMinimal change · temp 1.0 · anonymous13.55%1.147Same prompt rerun on the current backend

What changed

DeepSeek changed the backend serving the same deepseek-v4-flash name on July 31, 2026. The API label stayed the same; the experimental model did not.

What the reruns show

On V4-Flash-0731, anonymous V2 temperature-1 runs ranged from 12.23% to 16.37% CAGR (1.146 to 1.385 Sharpe), confirming material run-to-run variation across four independent anonymous runs. Minimal change produced 13.55% CAGR and 1.147 Sharpe. These current-epoch runs must not be pooled with Preview-era results.

Decision

Freeze Preview results as historical evidence. Use repeated V4-Flash-0731 controls for all new prompt comparisons, and do not combine the two epochs.

See all completed V4-Flash-0731 reruns

These are current-model runs only. The four anonymous V2 rows are independent repeats and make the run-to-run variance visible. The fourth run is stored in a misleadingly named ticker_visible folder, but its frozen config and cache both confirm anonymization.

Latest isolated experiment

Removing Market-Regime Information Hurt This Run

Current V4-Flash-0731 experiment using the same 251 dates and frozen harness. The treatment removes only the Market Regime glossary block and date-level regime value.

Removing Market Regime from both the feature glossary and date input produced 13.10% CAGR and 1.129 Sharpe, versus 15.20% and 1.290 for the fresh V2 control. That is a -2.10% CAGR change and -0.161 Sharpe change, with drawdown 1.14% deeper. This is one stochastic paired experiment and should be interpreted as current-epoch evidence, not as a confirmed replacement.

Latest prompt interaction

Shared-Control A / B / C / D Experiment

Development-only evidence using the same 251-date manifest, anonymous tickers, offset-3 60/60 → 30/30 construction, temperature 1, and unchanged accounting.

Control correction: A was collected once alongside B, frozen, and reused unchanged for C and D. The arms did not receive new controls each time.
ArmStatusDecisionAnnualized lift vs A95% paired CIP(lift > 0)Block stabilityBest-5%-trimmed liftLong liftShort lift
BComplete · 251/251RETAIN — development candidate+1.71%-1.49% to +4.98%84.64%75.0%-1.60%-0.40%+2.11%
CComplete · 251/251REJECT-0.90%-4.24% to +2.28%29.96%25.0%-3.88%-0.58%-0.31%
DIncomplete · 137/251PRUNE — interaction futility; not statistically rejectedNot computedNot computedNot computedNot computedNot computedNot computedNot computed

A — Shared Fresh Control

12.66% CAGR · 1.094 Sharpe · -8.76% max drawdown. This exact A artifact is the comparison anchor for B, C, and D.

B — De-prescribed Context

Exact prompt change: the old explanatory context was replaced with: “We are evaluating two pools of stocks that have experienced extreme 5-day moves, identifying those most likely to partially reverse over the next 5 trading days based on exhaustion, positioning, and regime signals.” The rule “Use the 0–100 range only when the supplied cross-section supports it. Do not manufacture separation” stayed unchanged.

14.54% CAGR · 1.195 Sharpe · -8.40% drawdown. Lift versus A was +1.71 points: longs -0.40, shorts +2.11. Paired hit rate was 52.59%, block stability 75%, P(lift > 0) 84.64%, and the 95% CI was -1.49 to +4.98 points.

Decision: retain as a development candidate because it materially improved the short sleeve—not because CAGR alone won. The best-5%-trimmed lift was -1.60 points, so it is promising but concentrated and unvalidated.

C — Proportional Score Separation

11.64% CAGR · 1.002 Sharpe · -10.37% drawdown. Lift versus A was -0.90 points: longs -0.58, shorts -0.31. Stability was 25%, P(lift > 0) 29.96%, CI -4.24 to +2.28, and trimmed lift -3.88 points.

Decision: reject. Forced proportional separation damaged both sides and worsened drawdown.

D — B × C Interaction

D combined B's neutral context with C's instruction: “Use the full 0–100 range. Two tickers that differ on a signal must receive distinct scores proportional to the signal's magnitude.” The feature glossary, task, strict JSON contract, and anonymous tickers stayed fixed.

D cached 137/251 dates through 2011-09-16 using 142 calls and 250,275 output tokens, then stopped on DeepSeek 402. No D return series exists, so no CAGR, lift, or statistical rejection is claimed.

Decision: prune for incomplete interaction futility: D's remaining proposition was that B could rescue C's already harmful rule. Preserve the cache.

How candidates are judged: CAGR is not the sole selection key. Short lift, long-side damage, chronological stability, concentration, paired uncertainty, drawdown, and weak-state behavior all matter. This explicitly carries forward the G0_02/G0_01 lesson and the autoresearch finding that a prompt can remain useful when it materially improves the short sleeve.
Validation status: B is retained only for further development; C is rejected; D is pruned incomplete. No 2015+ blind artifact was read or touched. Open the detailed thesis-package record.

Full prompt 
MEAN REVERSION DISCOVERY STAGE
Use only the provided point-in-time inputs to identify the strongest current
mean-reversion longs and shorts for the next rebalance horizon.
Treat tickers as opaque identifiers. Do not use outside knowledge, calendar
knowledge, or ticker familiarity.

MECHANICAL BASELINE:
- Compute mean_reversion_signal = -(trailing 5-trading-day return)
- Rank the full universe cross-sectionally by this signal
- LONG the highest signal names = biggest recent losers
- SHORT the lowest signal names = biggest recent winners
- Equal-weight each leg
- Ignore signal quality, regime context, and implementation details

ALPHA RULES:
1. Reversal quality first:
   Prefer names showing short-term exhaustion or stretch with a plausible snapback setup.
   Longs should look washed out, extended lower, and crowded to the downside.
   Shorts should look overheated, extended higher, and crowded to the upside.

2. Broad-universe discipline:
   Candidate pools come from the full ranked universe.
   Judge candidates only from mean-reversion rank, stretch/exhaustion, volatility,
   trend, range, momentum, and regime context.

__PIPELINE_CONTEXT__

3. Trend and regime awareness:
   Penalize fades that are fighting extremely strong medium-term continuation without
   any sign of exhaustion.
   In crisis or squeeze regimes, demand cleaner reversal evidence before fading extremes.

4. Loss memory:
   If similar prior loss cases are shown, treat them as cautionary analogs.
   Do not blindly avoid the trade, but require clearly better stretch/exhaustion quality
   than the failed analogs before repeating the setup.

OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT JSON):
{
  "long_candidates": [
    {"ticker": "AAPL", "edge_score": 88, "rationale": "Oversold stretch with snapback setup"},
    ...
  ],
  "short_candidates": [
    {"ticker": "XYZ", "edge_score": 84, "rationale": "Overbought extension with fade risk"},
    ...
  ],
  "reasoning": "2-3 sentence summary of the alpha slate",
  "regime_adjustment": "How the regime affected your candidate quality threshold"
}

Full prompt 
MEAN REVERSION SIGNAL RANKING STAGE
Use only the provided point-in-time D1/D10 mean-reversion candidate buckets.
Treat tickers as opaque identifiers. Do not use outside knowledge, calendar
knowledge, or ticker familiarity.

TASK:
- Rank names within each side by expected mean-reversion edge over the next holding horizon.
- Assign confidence_score for each name.
- confidence_score means: confidence that this name has a better mean-reversion edge
  than other names in the same D1/D10 bucket over the next holding horizon.
- confidence_score is not a position size and not a probability of positive return.

IMPORTANT:
- Do not construct a portfolio.
- Do not assign weights.
- Do not consider turnover, holdings, transaction costs, or implementation.
- Return every provided candidate exactly once on the same side.

OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT JSON):
{
  "long_rankings": [
    {"ticker": "AAPL", "rank": 1, "confidence_score": 88},
    ...
  ],
  "short_rankings": [
    {"ticker": "XYZ", "rank": 1, "confidence_score": 84},
    ...
  ],
  "reasoning": "1-2 sentence summary of the ranking criteria"
}

Full prompt 
MEAN-REVERSION RAW CONFIDENCE SCORING

CONTEXT
Mean reversion is the tendency of stocks that have moved sharply in one
direction to partially reverse over the next 5 trading days, especially
when the move shows signs of exhaustion (extreme indicators, volume
climax, range positioning). The strongest reversion setups combine an
extreme recent move with exhaustion signals and a supportive regime.

TASK
You are scoring two pools of stocks selected as mean-reversion candidates over
the next 5 trading days.

  LONG POOL  = biggest 5-day losers after Russell 1000, price, and ADV filters.
               Higher confidence means a stronger oversold snap-back edge
               versus other names in this same long pool.

  SHORT POOL = biggest 5-day winners after Russell 1000, price, and ADV filters.
               Higher confidence means a stronger overbought fade edge versus
               other names in this same short pool.

For each ticker in each pool, output a single SCORE from 0 to 100. In this
prompt, SCORE means:

  Confidence that this name has a better mean-reversion edge than other names
  in the same LONG or SHORT pool over the next 5 trading days.

Important interpretation rules:
- The score is not a position size.
- The score is not a probability of a positive return.
- The score is side-relative: compare long names only to long names, and short
  names only to short names.
- Use only the indicator columns provided. No outside knowledge, no ticker
  familiarity, no calendar awareness.
- Treat tickers as opaque identifiers.
- Use the full 0-100 range when the cross-section supports it.
- Do not produce ranks, weights, portfolio sizes, rationales, or commentary.

OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT JSON, no other text, no markdown fences):
{
  "long_scores":  [{"ticker": "AAPL", "score": 72}, {"ticker": "MSFT", "score": 58}, ...],
  "short_scores": [{"ticker": "XYZ",  "score": 84}, {"ticker": "ABC", "score": 61}, ...]
}
V1/Legacy bridge Tested, weaker than raw confidence in legacy comparison

MEAN-REVERSION RANK + CONFIDENCE SCORING

CONTEXT
Mean reversion is the tendency of stocks that have moved sharply in one
direction to partially reverse over the next 5 trading days, especially
when the move shows signs of exhaustion (extreme indicators, volume
climax, range positioning). The strongest reversion setups combine an
extreme recent move with exhaustion signals and a supportive regime.

TASK
You are ranking two pools of stocks selected as mean-reversion candidates over
the next 5 trading days.

  LONG POOL  = biggest 5-day losers after Russell 1000, price, and ADV filters.
               Rank 1 is the strongest oversold snap-back setup.

  SHORT POOL = biggest 5-day winners after Russell 1000, price, and ADV filters.
               Rank 1 is the strongest overbought fade setup.

For every ticker in each pool, output:
- rank: integer from 1 to N within that side, with no ties and no gaps.
- confidence_score: integer from 0 to 100 indicating confidence in that rank's
  mean-reversion edge versus other names in the same side.

Important interpretation rules:
- Rank long names only against other long names.
- Rank short names only against other short names.
- Do not rank across sides.
- Use only the indicator columns provided. No outside knowledge, no ticker
  familiarity, no calendar awareness.
- Treat tickers as opaque identifiers.
- Do not produce weights, portfolio sizes, rationales, or commentary.

OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT JSON, no other text, no markdown fences):
{
  "long_rankings":  [{"ticker": "AAPL", "rank": 1, "confidence_score": 82}, {"ticker": "MSFT", "rank": 2, "confidence_score": 74}, ...],
  "short_rankings": [{"ticker": "XYZ",  "rank": 1, "confidence_score": 79}, {"ticker": "ABC", "rank": 2, "confidence_score": 70}, ...]
}

Full prompt 
MEAN-REVERSION CANDIDATE SCORING

CONTEXT
Mean reversion is the tendency of stocks that have moved sharply in one
direction to partially reverse over the next 5 trading days, especially
when the move shows signs of exhaustion (extreme indicators, volume
climax, range positioning). The strongest reversion setups combine an
extreme recent move with exhaustion signals and a supportive regime.

FEATURE GLOSSARY

All values are computed using data available as of the rebalance date.
Scale note:
- MRL/MRS are 0-100 cross-sectional percentile scores.
- Trail5/MRSig are shown as percent returns.

Ticker
- Security identifier. In anonymized runs, this is a synthetic SEC_#### ID.

Trail5
- Stock's trailing 5-trading-day return, in percent.

MRSig
- Mean-reversion signal, equal to negative trailing-5-day return.

MRL
- Long-side extreme-move percentile.
- Computed as the cross-sectional percentile rank of MRSig across the valid universe.
- Higher MRL means higher MRSig, usually a bigger recent loser.

MRS
- Short-side extreme-move percentile.
- Computed as 100 - MRL.
- Higher MRS usually means a bigger recent winner.

RSI5
- 5-day RSI computed from closing prices.

Rng55
- Current close location inside the trailing 55-day high-low range.
- Approximately 0 = trailing 55-day low; approximately 1 = trailing 55-day high.

Vel/V
- Recent 10-trading-day return divided by recent 21-trading-day annualized volatility.

%MA100
- Percent distance from the 100-day moving average.

52WH
- Percent distance from trailing 52-week high.

Mom3m
- Trailing roughly 3-month return, in percent.

AutoC
- Autocorrelation of recent daily log returns at lag 5.
- Computed as the correlation between each recent daily return and the daily return 5 trading days earlier.

ADX
- 14-day Average Directional Index, trend strength only.

VolPctl
- Current stock realized-volatility percentile over 504 trading days. Higher = stock is in a high-vol regime.

Market Regime
- normal: Mid/default volatility regime.
- crisis: High volatility and SPY below 200-day MA.
- squeeze: High volatility and SPY above 200-day MA.
- grind_up: Low volatility and SPY above 200-day MA.
- slow_bleed: Low volatility and SPY below 200-day MA.

Important: MRL, MRS, MRSig, and Trail5 are mechanically linked views of the same recent 5-trading-day move, not four independent signals.

TASK
You are scoring two pools of stocks selected as mean-reversion candidates
over the next 5 trading days.

  LONG POOL  = the biggest 5-day losers in the Russell 1000 candidate universe.
               Looking for snap-back / oversold-bounce setups.

  SHORT POOL = the biggest 5-day winners in the Russell 1000 candidate universe.
               Looking for fade / overbought-reversal setups.

For each ticker in each pool, output a single SCORE (0-100):

  LONG POOL:
    100 = strongest snap-back setup (most oversold, most exhausted seller,
          most primed for an upward reversion over the next 5 trading days)
    50  = average candidate in this pool
    0   = weakest snap-back setup (least likely to bounce despite the move)

  SHORT POOL:
    100 = strongest fade setup (most overbought, most exhausted buyer,
          most primed for a downward reversion over the next 5 trading days)
    50  = average candidate in this pool
    0   = weakest fade setup (least likely to reverse despite the move)

RULES
- Score every ticker in both pools. Do not skip any.
- Use the full 0-100 range. Do not bunch scores around 50.
- Use only the indicator columns provided. No outside knowledge, no
  calendar awareness, no ticker familiarity.
- Treat tickers as opaque identifiers.
- Do not pick top names, build a portfolio, assign weights, write
  rationales, or provide commentary of any kind.

OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT JSON, no other text, no markdown fences):
{
  "long_scores":  [{"ticker": "SEC_0001", "score": 72}, {"ticker": "SEC_0002", "score": 58}],
  "short_scores": [{"ticker": "SEC_0061", "score": 84}, {"ticker": "SEC_0062", "score": 61}]
}

MEAN-REVERSION CANDIDATE SCORING

CONTEXT
Mean reversion is the tendency of stocks that have moved sharply in one
direction to partially reverse over the next 5 trading days, especially
when the move shows signs of exhaustion (extreme indicators, volume
climax, range positioning). The strongest reversion setups combine an
extreme recent move with exhaustion signals and a supportive regime.

FEATURE GLOSSARY

All values are computed using data available as of the rebalance date.
Scale note:
- MRL/MRS are 0-100 cross-sectional percentile scores.
- Trail5/MRSig are shown as percent returns.

Ticker
- Security identifier. In anonymized runs, this is a synthetic SEC_#### ID.

Trail5
- Stock's trailing 5-trading-day return, in percent.

MRSig
- Mean-reversion signal, equal to negative trailing-5-day return.

MRL
- Long-side extreme-move percentile.
- Computed as the cross-sectional percentile rank of MRSig across the valid universe.
- Higher MRL means higher MRSig, usually a bigger recent loser.

MRS
- Short-side extreme-move percentile.
- Computed as 100 - MRL.
- Higher MRS usually means a bigger recent winner.

RSI5
- 5-day RSI computed from closing prices.

Rng55
- Current close location inside the trailing 55-day high-low range.
- Approximately 0 = trailing 55-day low; approximately 1 = trailing 55-day high.

Vel/V
- Recent 10-trading-day return divided by recent 21-trading-day annualized volatility.

%MA100
- Percent distance from the 100-day moving average.

52WH
- Percent distance from trailing 52-week high.

Mom3m
- Trailing roughly 3-month return, in percent.

AutoC
- Autocorrelation of recent daily log returns at lag 5.
- Computed as the correlation between each recent daily return and the daily return 5 trading days earlier.

ADX
- 14-day Average Directional Index, trend strength only.

VolPctl
- Current stock realized-volatility percentile over 504 trading days. Higher = stock is in a high-vol regime.

Market Regime
- normal: Mid/default volatility regime.
- crisis: High volatility and SPY below 200-day MA.
- squeeze: High volatility and SPY above 200-day MA.
- grind_up: Low volatility and SPY above 200-day MA.
- slow_bleed: Low volatility and SPY below 200-day MA.

Important: MRL, MRS, MRSig, and Trail5 are mechanically linked views of the same recent 5-trading-day move, not four independent signals.

TASK
You are scoring two pools of stocks selected as mean-reversion candidates
over the next 5 trading days.

  LONG POOL  = the biggest 5-day losers in the Russell 1000 candidate universe.
               Looking for snap-back / oversold-bounce setups.

  SHORT POOL = the biggest 5-day winners in the Russell 1000 candidate universe.
               Looking for fade / overbought-reversal setups.

For each ticker in each pool, output a single SCORE (0-100):

  LONG POOL:
    100 = strongest snap-back setup (most oversold, most exhausted seller,
          most primed for an upward reversion over the next 5 trading days)
    50  = average candidate in this pool
    0   = weakest snap-back setup (least likely to bounce despite the move)

  SHORT POOL:
    100 = strongest fade setup (most overbought, most exhausted buyer,
          most primed for a downward reversion over the next 5 trading days)
    50  = average candidate in this pool
    0   = weakest fade setup (least likely to reverse despite the move)

RULES
- Score every ticker in both pools. Do not skip any.
- Use the 0-100 range only when the supplied cross-section supports it. Do not manufacture separation.
- Use only the indicator columns provided. No outside knowledge, no
  calendar awareness, no ticker familiarity.
- Treat tickers as opaque identifiers.
- Do not pick top names, build a portfolio, assign weights, write
  rationales, or provide commentary of any kind.

OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT JSON, no other text, no markdown fences):
{
  "long_scores":  [{"ticker": "SEC_0001", "score": 72}, {"ticker": "SEC_0002", "score": 58}],
  "short_scores": [{"ticker": "SEC_0061", "score": 84}, {"ticker": "SEC_0062", "score": 61}]
}

MEAN-REVERSION CANDIDATE SCORING

DATA SCOPE

All inputs are point-in-time values available as of the rebalance.
Use only the supplied fields. Do not use outside knowledge, issuer
familiarity, calendar priors, or facts inferred from a security identifier.

FEATURE GLOSSARY

Scale:
- MRL and MRS are 0-100 cross-sectional percentile scores.
- Trail5 and MRSig are percent returns.
- A value shown as nan is unavailable. Do not treat nan as zero,
  neutral, favorable, or unfavorable.

Ticker
- Security identifier. In anonymized runs this is a synthetic SEC_#### ID.

Trail5
- Trailing 5-trading-day stock return, in percent.

MRSig
- Negative Trail5.

MRL
- Cross-sectional percentile rank of MRSig across the valid universe.
- Higher MRL usually identifies a larger recent loser.

MRS
- 100 minus MRL.
- Higher MRS usually identifies a larger recent winner.

RSI5
- 5-day RSI computed from closing prices.

Rng55
- Current close location within the trailing 55-day high-low range.
- Approximately 0 is the trailing low and approximately 1 is the trailing high.

Vel/V
- Recent 10-trading-day return divided by recent 21-trading-day
  annualized volatility.

%MA100
- Percent distance from the 100-day moving average.

52WH
- Percent distance from the trailing 52-week high.

Mom3m
- Trailing approximately 3-month return, in percent.

AutoC
- Autocorrelation of recent daily log returns at lag 5.

ADX
- 14-day Average Directional Index; trend strength only.

VolPctl
- Current realized-volatility percentile over 504 trading days.

Market Regime
- normal: middle/default volatility regime.
- crisis: high volatility with SPY below its 200-day moving average.
- squeeze: high volatility with SPY above its 200-day moving average.
- grind_up: low volatility with SPY above its 200-day moving average.
- slow_bleed: low volatility with SPY below its 200-day moving average.

Trail5, MRSig, MRL, and MRS are mechanically linked views of one
recent five-day move. Treat them as one feature family, not as four
independent confirmations. The recent move remains relevant.

TASK

Score every ticker by its relative mean-reversion setup over the next
5 trading days.

LONG POOL
- Recent five-day losers.
- Higher score means a stronger expected upward snap-back setup relative
  to other names in the LONG pool.

SHORT POOL
- Recent five-day winners.
- Higher score means a stronger expected downward fade setup relative
  to other names in the SHORT pool.

Use the remaining supplied features as context. They may confirm,
contradict, or add no useful information to the core recent move.
Do not apply fixed feature directions, fixed feature weights, hard
regime gates, or mechanical feature-counting rules.

SCORE INTERPRETATION

- Score is an integer from 0 to 100.
- 100 means strongest relative setup within that side.
- 50 means approximately the middle of that side's candidate pool.
- 0 means weakest relative setup within that side.
- Scores are ordinal side-relative signals.
- Scores are not probabilities, expected returns, confidence intervals,
  portfolio weights, or position sizes.
- Do not compare LONG scores with SHORT scores.
- Use broad score separation only when the supplied cross-section supports it.
  Do not manufacture a full range or equal spacing.

RULES

- Return every provided ticker exactly once.
- Keep every ticker on its original side.
- Do not add tickers or omit tickers.
- Do not select a final portfolio or assign weights.
- Do not consider holdings, turnover, transaction costs, or implementation.
- Do not return rationales, labels, explanations, commentary, or markdown.
- Return only valid JSON with exactly the required top-level and entry keys.

OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT JSON, NO OTHER TEXT)

{
  "long_scores": [
    {"ticker": "SEC_0001", "score": 72},
    {"ticker": "SEC_0002", "score": 58}
  ],
  "short_scores": [
    {"ticker": "SEC_0061", "score": 84},
    {"ticker": "SEC_0062", "score": 61}
  ]
}

MEAN-REVERSION DISCRIMINATIVE SCORE PROMPT V2.1

CONTEXT
The candidate pools are already selected by extreme 5-trading-day moves.
Trail5 and RSI5 are useful context, but they are not enough by themselves.
Your job is to score the incremental quality of the mean-reversion setup
beyond the obvious recent move and short-term overbought/oversold reading.

TASK
Score two side-relative pools over the next 5 trading days.

  LONG POOL  = biggest 5-day losers after Russell 1000, price, and ADV filters.
               Higher score means the loser looks more like exhausted selling
               and less like a falling knife / continuation breakdown.

  SHORT POOL = biggest 5-day winners after Russell 1000, price, and ADV filters.
               Higher score means the winner looks more like exhausted buying
               and less like a squeeze / momentum continuation.

For each ticker in each pool, output one SCORE from 0 to 100.

SCORING PRINCIPLES
- Start from the fact that every name is already an extreme Trail5 candidate.
- Do not simply rank by Trail5 magnitude or RSI5 extremity.
- Use Trail5 and RSI5 as baseline context, then discriminate using the other
  columns to judge exhaustion versus continuation risk.
- Use the full 0-100 range when the cross-section supports it.
- Compare long names only to long names, and short names only to short names.

LONG-SIDE GUIDANCE
- Reward losers where secondary features support snap-back/exhaustion,
  especially AutoC, MRSig, and Rng55.
- Trail5 and RSI5 can support a high score only when secondary features also
  look favorable.
- Penalize names where the move looks like persistent downside or unstable
  high-volatility continuation, especially weak MRS, high VolPctl, poor Mom3m,
  poor 52WH, or other continuation-like evidence.

SHORT-SIDE GUIDANCE
- Reward winners where secondary features support overbought fade/exhaustion,
  especially Vel/V, %MA100, MRL, and MRS.
- Trail5 and RSI5 can support a high score only when secondary features also
  look favorable.
- Penalize names where the move looks like squeeze/continuation risk,
  especially unfavorable MRSig, AutoC, VolPctl, 52WH, or other continuation-like
  evidence.

RULES
- Use only the indicator columns provided. No outside knowledge, no ticker
  familiarity, no calendar awareness.
- Treat tickers as opaque identifiers.
- Do not produce ranks, weights, portfolio sizes, rationales, labels, or
  commentary.
- Return every provided ticker exactly once on its original side.

OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT JSON, no other text, no markdown fences):
{
  "long_scores":  [{"ticker": "SEC_0001", "score": 72}, {"ticker": "SEC_0002", "score": 58}],
  "short_scores": [{"ticker": "SEC_0061", "score": 84}, {"ticker": "SEC_0062", "score": 61}]
}
V2.3 Tested, slight Trail5 lift but weaker than V2 score prompt

CONTEXT
Mean reversion is the tendency of stocks that have moved sharply in one
direction to partially reverse over the next 5 trading days, especially
when the move shows signs of exhaustion (extreme indicators, volume
climax, range positioning). The strongest reversion setups combine an
extreme recent move with exhaustion signals and a supportive regime.

FEATURE GLOSSARY

All values are computed using data available as of the rebalance date.
Scale note:
- MRL/MRS are 0-100 cross-sectional percentile scores.
- Trail5/MRSig are shown as percent returns.

Ticker
- Security identifier. In anonymized runs, this is a synthetic SEC_#### ID.

Trail5
- Stock's trailing 5-trading-day return, in percent.

MRSig
- Mean-reversion signal, equal to negative trailing-5-day return.

MRL
- Long-side extreme-move percentile.
- Computed as the cross-sectional percentile rank of MRSig across the valid universe.
- Higher MRL means higher MRSig, usually a bigger recent loser.

MRS
- Short-side extreme-move percentile.
- Computed as 100 - MRL.
- Higher MRS usually means a bigger recent winner.

RSI5
- 5-day RSI computed from closing prices.

Rng55
- Current close location inside the trailing 55-day high-low range.
- Approximately 0 = trailing 55-day low; approximately 1 = trailing 55-day high.

Vel/V
- Recent 10-trading-day return divided by recent 21-trading-day annualized volatility.

%MA100
- Percent distance from the 100-day moving average.

52WH
- Percent distance from trailing 52-week high.

Mom3m
- Trailing roughly 3-month return, in percent.

AutoC
- Autocorrelation of recent daily log returns at lag 5.
- Computed as the correlation between each recent daily return and the daily return 5 trading days earlier.

ADX
- 14-day Average Directional Index, trend strength only.

VolPctl
- Current stock realized-volatility percentile over 504 trading days. Higher = stock is in a high-vol regime.

Market Regime
- normal: Mid/default volatility regime.
- crisis: High volatility and SPY below 200-day MA.
- squeeze: High volatility and SPY above 200-day MA.
- grind_up: Low volatility and SPY above 200-day MA.
- slow_bleed: Low volatility and SPY below 200-day MA.

Important: MRL, MRS, MRSig, and Trail5 are mechanically linked views of the same recent 5-trading-day move, not four independent signals.

  LONG POOL  = the biggest 5-day losers in the Russell 1000 candidate universe.
               Looking for snap-back / oversold-bounce setups.

  SHORT POOL = the biggest 5-day winners in the Russell 1000 candidate universe.
               Looking for fade / overbought-reversal setups.

For LONG POOL:
  long_ranking[0] = strongest snap-back setup
  long_ranking[-1] = weakest snap-back setup

For SHORT POOL:
  short_ranking[0] = strongest fade setup
  short_ranking[-1] = weakest fade setup

RULES FOR V2.3
- Include every ticker in each pool exactly once.
- Keep each ticker on its original side.

V2.5 BALANCED OUTCOME MEMORY

The user table contains three additional point-in-time historical-analog fields:
- HistN: number of prior same-side analogs used, from 0 to 20.
- HistWin: percent of those analogs with a profitable side-adjusted 5-day C2C outcome.
- HistRet: average side-adjusted 5-day C2C return of those analogs, in percent.

For LONG candidates, positive HistRet means analogous stocks subsequently rose.
For SHORT candidates, positive HistRet means analogous stocks subsequently fell.
The analog set includes both wins and losses and never includes an outcome that
was unavailable on the rebalance date. These fields are contextual evidence,
not guarantees. Do not mechanically replace the technical setup with HistWin or
HistRet. Keep the original V2 0-100 score definition and output schema unchanged.
Prompt A/B

V2 vs V2.1: What Actually Changed

V2.1 used the same harness, anonymized tickers, offset 3, 60/60 -> 30/30 book, score-only schema, and gross fixed 5-day C2C replay. The performance delta came from prompt-driven score behavior.

DimensionV2V2.1Lesson
Core askScore mean-reversion setup strength.Score incremental setup quality beyond Trail5 / RSI5.V2.1 demoted the core reversal signal instead of lightly broadening attention.
Trail5 / RSI5 treatmentPart of the general extreme-indicator context.Explicitly says they are not enough and should not be simply ranked.The model moved away from the strongest known signal.
Risk languageMinimal caution language.Falling knife, continuation, squeeze, high-vol continuation warnings.The prompt became too conservative for an intentionally ugly reversal book.
Feature steeringBroad: extreme move + exhaustion + volume/range/regime.Harder steering toward named secondary features.The diagnostics were overfit into the prompt.
Harness / schema60/60 -> 30/30, offset 3, gross fixed 5-day C2C, score-only JSON.Same.The performance delta is from prompt/score behavior, not accounting or replay mechanics.
Working interpretation: V2.1 was not a light reminder to inspect more columns. It became a hard instruction to find incremental evidence beyond Trail5/RSI5, which pushed the model away from the core reversal exposure.

Historical Preview-Epoch V2.6 And Minimal-Change Results

Historical 2009-2014 development runs from the prior model epoch. They remain useful evidence about Preview behavior, but they are not directly comparable with V4-Flash-0731. The minimal treatment changes one range-separation instruction; V2.6 changes the full prompt bundle.

At temperature 1.0, minimal change reached 16.77% CAGR (+0.22pp versus temperature 0.6) with 1.400 Sharpe and -7.43% max drawdown. Temperature 0.6 retained slightly higher Sharpe (1.410) and IC (0.0373 versus 0.0318). Temperature 1.0 remains the preferred challenger setting. Temperature 1.5 fell to 12.23% CAGR, 1.091 Sharpe, and -10.03% max drawdown (-0.93pp versus Trail5); it also produced a rejected score of 800 before completing through a clean cache resume. V2.6 produced 12.27% CAGR and 1.057 Sharpe, trailing frozen V2 by -1.31pp. A second independent temperature-0.6 run produced 13.70% CAGR and 1.133 Sharpe (-2.84pp versus the first temperature-0.6 run), showing material sampling variation.

2015-2019 Glossary Ablation

Both prompt variants use the same dates, candidate pools, accounting, and byte-identical Trail5 artifacts. This separates prompt wording from portfolio construction.

Original equal-weight 30/30: no glossary returned 0.9% CAGR versus 1.6% for Trail5 (-0.7% lift); glossary returned 2.6% (+1.1% lift). Glossary therefore added +1.7% versus no glossary on the primary book. No glossary was stronger on five of the six concentrated constructions, so the effect depends on the wrapper.

Temperature Check

DeepSeek Chat temperature can change score stability and ranking spread. Temp 0 and temp 0.6 now form a clean current-prompt control; the other completed temperatures extend the sensitivity sweep.

Control complete: temp 0.6 produced 13.6% CAGR versus 13.5% at temp 0 (+0.12pp), with Sharpe 1.153 versus 1.159 (-0.006). Selected-book overlap was 83.2% and common-name score-rank correlation was 0.934. The economic result is effectively unchanged, so temperature 0 is not being credited with a lucky one-off advantage.

V2.2 Adjustment Distribution And Outcomes

Why the contextual-adjustment run underperformed: the model's -3..+3 buckets did not consistently map to better side-adjusted P&L, especially on shorts.

Read: positive Side P&L is good for the intended side. V2.2 had some useful long-side separation, but positive short adjustments often selected stocks that rose, which hurts shorts.

Side-Specific Process Hybrids

This tests whether longs and shorts should use different selection processes, using existing ticker-visible V2 and V2.2 caches only.

Read: the original V2 long ranking remains the best long process. The only small improvement came from using the V2.2 adjustment as a short-side overlay on top of V2 scores, but the lift is small enough to treat as a prompt-design clue, not a new winner yet.

V2.5 Point-In-Time Memory Audit

V2.5 keeps the glossary-backed V2 score definition and adds only three fields: HistN, HistWin, and HistRet. Retrieval uses prior same-side candidates, excludes same-ticker history, caps representation from any one rebalance date, and never uses an outcome unavailable on the signal date.

Candidate-date rows30,120HistN / HistWin / HistRet summaries available for audit
Neighbor links595,200Auditable point-in-time analog assignments
Historical cases30,107Prior candidates with realized side-adjusted five-day outcomes
Status: Memory-ready V2.5 produced 10.5% CAGR and 1.08 Sharpe, with -2.6pp CAGR lift versus Trail5 and -1.7pp versus the fresh V2 control.

Why V2.5 Failed: Zero-Token Memory Postmortem

The diagnostic asks whether HistWin/HistRet actually predicted future side-adjusted returns, whether V2.5 reacted to those fields, and whether the names it added beat the fresh-V2 names it dropped. Statistical intervals resample whole rebalance dates.

Conclusion: Memory changed scores materially (mean absolute memory-to-score rank correlation 0.290), but its strongest absolute correlation with future side-adjusted return was only 0.029. V2.5-added names versus dropped names produced -0.01% per rebalance, with a 95% interval from -0.22% to 0.20%. Do not fund another memory prompt yet. The analog fields moved scores but did not show a stable negative tail. Open the full postmortem.

Cache Forensics

What We Can Learn From The Cache

The cache and replay files let us inspect candidate-level score changes, selected-name overlap, feature emphasis, structured labels, and week-by-week damage without rerunning the LLM.

Glossary Changes Where The Edge Lives

Finding: On equal-weight 30/30, glossary CAGR was 2.6% versus 0.9% without the glossary and 1.6% for Trail5. No glossary was stronger on 5 of 6 concentrated constructions.

Implication: Working hypothesis: the glossary helps the diversified primary book but may smooth or overthink the highest-conviction ordering. In plain terms, glossary may help diversification while no glossary may help concentration. Because every wrapper was examined on the same 2015-2019 period, treat this as a hypothesis to validate, not a settled conclusion.

backtest_results/llm_reversal_signal_v2_2009_2014/phase2_validation_tracker/phase2_scorecard.csv

Selection Replacement

Finding: V2-only longs averaged -8.5% Trail5; V2.1-only longs averaged -6.0%.

Implication: V2.1 replaced uglier/extreme reversal names with cleaner, less extreme names.

backtest_results/llm_reversal_signal_v2_1_anon_2009_2014/cache_forensics/selection_bucket_outcomes.csv

Short Book Read

Finding: V2-only shorts had avg fwd C2C -0.1%; V2.1-only shorts had 0.3%.

Implication: Short-side changes were less catastrophic than long-side changes, but did not create enough lift.

backtest_results/llm_reversal_signal_v2_1_anon_2009_2014/cache_forensics/selection_bucket_outcomes.csv

What V2 Scored

Finding: Long top correlations: MRL (0.49), MRSig (0.36), VolPctl (0.13). Short top correlations: MRS (0.54), RSI5 (0.38), Vel/V (0.38).

Implication: V2 naturally kept a strong reversal-ranking shape while still using context.

backtest_results/llm_reversal_signal_v2_1_anon_2009_2014/cache_forensics/score_feature_correlations.csv

What V2.1 Scored

Finding: Long top correlations: %MA100 (0.59), Rng55 (0.58), Mom3m (0.57). Short top correlations: Mom3m (0.13), Rng55 (0.13), %MA100 (0.13).

Implication: V2.1 over-rotated into secondary cleanliness features and lost the core reversal exposure.

backtest_results/llm_reversal_signal_v2_1_anon_2009_2014/cache_forensics/score_feature_correlations.csv

Structured Labels

Finding: SHORT overbought reversal: n=14248, avg fwd 0.2%; LONG oversold reversal: n=13713, avg fwd 0.8%; LONG mixed signals: n=678, avg fwd -0.0%

Implication: Labels are useful for debugging feature emphasis, but not proof of hidden reasoning.

backtest_results/llm_reversal_signal_v2_1_anon_2009_2014/cache_forensics/structured_label_outcomes.csv

Weekly Damage Pattern

Finding: Mean V2.1 minus V2 weekly LS C2C delta: -0.3%; long overlap 28.0%, short overlap 49.2%.

Implication: The prompt changed the book materially, especially on the long side.

DATE/POOL: 2009-01-06|pool60

We are asked to score each ticker in both pools on a 0-100 scale based on mean-reversion potential. The long pool is losers, looking for snap-back; the short pool is winners, looking for fade. The scoring should use the provided indicators only, no outside knowledge.

Key indicators for mean reversion:
- For long (oversold): The bigger the 5-day decline (Trail5 magnitude), the more oversold. But also need signs of exhaustion: RSI5 very low (below 30 is oversold), Rng55 (range of 55 days) - if price is near the low of the range (low Rng55), that might indicate further downside? Actually for reversion, we want signs that the move is exhausted. Typically, a very low RSI5 (<30) indicates oversold. Also, volume climax: high VolPctl (volume percentile) relative to recent volume suggests capitulation. Also, positive autocorrelation? Actually AutoC (autocorrelation of returns) - negative autocorrelation might mean the trend is weakening? But the problem says "exhaustion signals" - could be velocity (Vel/V) - if it's very negative, it might be accelerating, but we want deceleration. The MRSig (MRSignal) is the magnitude of the move? Actually MRL and MRS are mean reversion likelihood and strength? The column names: MRL (Mean Reversion Likelihood?), MRS (Mean Reversion Strength?), Trail5 (5-day trailing return), MRSig (sign of MRS?), RSI5 (5-day RSI), Rng55 (55-day range position, 0-1? Probably 1 is high of range, 0 is low of range), Vel/V (velocity/volume?), %MA100 (percent from 100-day MA), 52WH (52-week high %? Actually it's % from 52-week high? Negative means below high), Mom3m (3-month momentum), AutoC (autocorrelation of returns), ADX (average directional index, measures trend strength), VolPctl (volume percentile).

For short pool (overbought): similar but opposite. Trail5 is large positive, RSI5 is high (100 for many), Rng55 high (near top of range), VolPctl high indicates volume climax on the upside? Also, Vel/V (velocity/volume) positive might indicate acceleration? But we want exhaustion.

We need to score each ticker relative to its pool. Since the pools are the 60 biggest losers/winners, the magnitude of Trail5 varies. For the long pool, the biggest losers (largest negative Trail5) are more oversold, but also need exhaustion signals. The worst losers might still have momentum, so we need to incorporate RSI5, Rng55, VolPctl, AutoC, ADX.

Given the complexity, we need a systematic way to combine indicators into a score. Since we cannot use outside knowledge, we must use the data. A common approach is to standardize each indicator and combine with weights or use a heuristic. But we have 120 tickers to score, so we need to compute scores efficiently.

We can think of a composite scoring formula. For long pool, higher score = stronger snap-back setup. So we want:
- More negative Trail5 (larger magnitude) -> higher score
- Lower RSI5 -> higher score (more oversold)
- Lower Rng55 (closer to 0, near low of range) -> higher score? Actually if price is near the low of its 55-day range, that might indicate it's at a support? But for mean reversion, we want the price to be at extreme levels. Rng55: 0 means at 55-day low, 1 means at 55-day high. For long, we want low Rng55 (near 0) because it's at the bottom of the range. Positive velocity? Actually Vel/V: negative might indicate selling pressure. If it's very negative, it might be oversold. But we also need signs of exhaustion: if volume is high (VolPctl high), that could be capitulation. Also, if ADX is low, that means trend is weak, better for mean reversion? Actually ADX measures trend strength. For mean reversion, we want the trend to be exhausted, so a high ADX might indicate strong trend that could continue. But if ADX is very high, it might be a climax? Actually in mean reversion, we often look for a drop in ADX after a strong move. But here we only have current ADX. A high ADX (>40) indicates strong trend, which might not be good for reversion. So lower ADX is better for reversion? But also, if ADX is very high, it could indicate a blow-off move that might reverse. So it's ambiguous. We'll need to use the given indicators as per the context: "extreme indicators, volume climax, range positioning".

The problem says: "The strongest reversion setups combine an extreme recent move with exhaustion signals and a supportive regime." So we need to capture:
1. Extremeness of move: magnitude of Trail5 (for long, large negative; for short, large positive).
2. Exhaustion signals:
   - RSI5 extreme (low for long, high for short)
   - Volume climax: VolPctl high (volume relative to recent)
   - Range positioning: Rng55 extreme (low for long, high for short)
   - Velocity/volume: perhaps a deceleration? Vel/V: if it's large in magnitude but with sign? For long, a very negative Vel/V might indicate strong selling, but we want the selling to be exhausting. If Vel/V is very negative, it might still be accelerating. But perhaps if it's less negative relative to the move, it might show deceleration. Actually Vel/V is defined as velocity/volume? Not sure. We'll need to interpret.
   - Autocorrelation: if AutoC is negative, it might indicate a tendency to reverse? Actually negative autocorrelation means returns are negatively correlated with previous returns, so if it's negative, it might mean the trend is not continuing.
   - ADX: trend strength. High ADX might mean strong trend, but sometimes a very high ADX can also indicate a climax.
3. Supportive regime: the market regime is given as "normal", so no adjustment.

Given the complexity, we can create a scoring model where we normalize each relevant indicator to 0-1 and combine. But we need to output a score for each ticker, and we must use the full 0-100 range. Since we have 60 tickers per pool, we can rank them and assign scores based on composite rank.

Simpler approach: Assign a composite score based on a weighted combination of key indicators. For the long pool, we want:
- Trail5 (negative magnitude): higher absolute value better -> use -Trail5 (positive) or rank.
- RSI5: lower is better -> use 100-RSI5? Actually RSI5 ranges 0-100. For long, we want low RSI5. So we can use (100-RSI5) as a component.
- Rng55: lower is better (near 0 means at low of range) -> use (1-Rng55) to score high when near low.
- VolPctl: higher is better (volume climax) -> VolPctl itself.
- AutoC: negative is good? Negative autocorrelation suggests reversal? Actually if AutoC is negative, it means that if yesterday's return was positive, today's tends to be negative, so it's a mean-reverting signal. But we don't know the lag. For long, a negative AutoC might be good. We'll use -AutoC if it's negative? Actually we want high score when AutoC is negative. So we can use ( -AutoC ) but if AutoC is positive, that's bad. So we can cap at 0 or use a transform.
- ADX: lower is better? Or high might be climax? I'll assume lower is better because mean reversion works best in non-trending markets. So we can use (100-ADX) as component, but ADX max is usually 100. But ADX in data seems to range 10-70. So lower ADX -> higher score.
- Vel/V: for long pool, Vel/V is negative for losers. If Vel/V is very negative, it might indicate strong selling. But we want exhaustion, so perhaps a less negative Vel/V suggests selling is tapering? Actually if the stock has dropped a lot but the velocity (price change relative to volume) is not that extreme, it might be a more orderly decline. I think for mean reversion, we want the velocity to be very negative (capitulation) because that indicates panic selling. So more negative Vel/V could be good? But it's ambiguous. I'll include it as absolute value? Actually for long pool, we want strong selling pressure to potentially reverse, so a very negative Vel/V (large in magnitude) might be good. So we can use -Vel/V (if Vel/V negative, -Vel/V positive) as a component.

Similarly, for short pool, we want:
- Trail5: large positive -> higher score (so Trail5 itself)
- RSI5: high -> use RSI5 directly
- Rng55: high -> use Rng55 directly (near 1)
- VolPctl: high -> VolPctl
- AutoC: if positive, might be continuation? Actually for short, we want reversal, so negative AutoC might be good (mean reversion). So same as long, we want negative AutoC.
- ADX: low is better for reversal? Or high might indicate climax? I'll assume low is better.
- Vel/V: positive for winners. We want it very positive (buying climax) so use Vel/V directly.

But we need to combine these into a single score. Since we have multiple indicators, we can standardize each to a 0-1 scale within the pool, then sum with equal weights or with some heuristic weights. To avoid complexity, I'll use a simple ranking method: For each indicator, rank the tickers within the pool (1 to 60, 1 being best for reversion, 60 being worst). Then sum the ranks (lower sum is better) and then convert to a 0-100 score (e.g., highest score for lowest sum). This ensures we use the full range.

Let's define for long pool the "good direction" for each indicator:
- Trail5: more negative is better. So rank by Trail5 ascending (most negative gets rank 1)
- RSI5: lower is better. Rank ascending.
- Rng55: lower is better (near 0). Rank ascending.
- VolPctl: higher is better. Rank descending (highest gets rank 1)
- AutoC: we want negative. But AutoC can be positive or negative. We want more negative better. So rank by AutoC ascending (most negative gets rank 1)
- ADX: lower is better. Rank ascending.
- Vel/V: more negative is better (large magnitude negative). Rank ascending.

Similarly for short pool:
- Trail5: larger positive better. Rank descending.
- RSI5: higher better. Rank descending.
- Rng55: higher better. Rank descending.
- VolPctl: higher better. Rank descending.
- AutoC: more negative better. Rank ascending.
- ADX: lower better. Rank ascending.
- Vel/V: more positive better. Rank descending.

Then compute total rank sum for each ticker (lower sum = better mean reversion setup). Then rescale to 0-100: min rank sum gets 100, max rank sum gets 0. Normalize linearly.

We need to handle missing values (like nan in VolPctl for some short pool). For SDOCQ, VolPctl is nan; for PCXCQ, VolPctl nan; for CPN, 52WH nan and VolPctl nan. For those, we cannot include VolPctl in rank; we'll ignore that indicator for them? Or we can assign a neutral rank (30.5 average)? To avoid bias, we'll compute ranks only for available indicators but then average the rank? Better to exclude that indicator from the sum for that ticker and then normalize the sum by the number of indicators used. But simpler: since only a few have missing, we can impute the median rank (30.5) for missing indicators. I'll impute median rank for missing to keep the total sum comparable.

Also for short pool, SDOCQ has nan for VolPctl, but we have other indicators. We'll assign rank 30.5 for that indicator.

Now, we need to implement this. Since I'm doing this manually in text, I'll need to compute ranks for each pool. That's 60 tickers per pool, 7 indicators each, so 420 rankings per pool. That's tedious but possible with careful work. However, as an AI, I can simulate the process by reasoning about typical values. But the instruction says "Use only the indicator columns provided." So I must compute based on data.

Given the length of the data, I'll attempt to approximate scores by looking at the data and pattern. But to be accurate, I should compute ranks. Since this is a text interaction, I'll need to output the JSON.

Alternative: I can use a weighted formula without ranking, but I need to ensure the scores are spread. For simplicity, I'll use the following composite score for long:

For each ticker, compute:
RawScore = ( -Trail5 / max(-Trail5) ) * w1 + ( (100-RSI5)/100 ) * w2 + ( (1-Rng55) ) * w3 + ( VolPctl/100 ) * w4 + ( -AutoC normalized ) * w5 + ( (100-ADX)/100 ) * w6 + ( -Vel/V / max(-Vel/V) ) * w7
Where w1..w7 are weights that sum to 1, but I can use equal weights (1/7). Then scale RawScore to 0-100 by min-max.

Similarly for short:
RawScore = ( Trail5/max(Trail5) ) * w1 + ( RSI5/100 ) * w2 + ( Rng55 ) * w3 + ( VolPctl/100 ) * w4 + ( -AutoC normalized ) * w5 + ( (100-ADX)/100 ) * w6 + ( Vel/V / max(Vel/V) ) * w7

But this requires normalizing each indicator to 0-1 relative to the pool max. The max values for each pool can be taken from data.

Let's extract the ranges:

For long pool:
- Trail5: from -18.413 to 0.169. Max magnitude: | -18.413 | = 18.413. So -Trail5/18.413 gives 0-1.
- RSI5: from 0.000 to 51.111. So (100-RSI5)/100: for RSI5=0, score=1; for 51.111, score=0.4889.
- Rng55: from 0.007 to 0.911. So (1-Rng55): for 0.007->0.993, for 0.911->0.089.
- VolPctl: from 31.746 to 96.230. Normalize by 100: VolPctl/100.
- AutoC: from -0.584 to 0.211. We want negative values high. We can normalize so that -AutoC ranges from -0.211 to 0.584. To get 0-1, we can do ( -AutoC - min(-AutoC) ) / (max(-AutoC) - min(-AutoC)). But simpler: we can use a sigmoid or just treat as raw. Since I'm doing a linear combination, I'll normalize to 0-1: min(-AutoC) = -0.211? Actually -AutoC: for AutoC=-0.584, -AutoC=0.584; for AutoC=0.211, -AutoC=-0.211. So the range is from -0.211 to 0.584. We can shift: ( -AutoC + 0.211 ) / (0.584+0.211) = ( -AutoC + 0.211 ) / 0.795. This gives 0-1.
- ADX: from 10.860 to 53.495. So (100-ADX)/100 ranges from (89.14/100)=0.8914 to (46.505/100)=0.4651.
- Vel/V: from -0.248 to 0.183. For long, we want negative Vel/V. So -Vel/V ranges from -0.183 to 0.248. We can normalize: ( -Vel/V - min(-Vel/V) ) / (max - min). min(-Vel/V) = -0.183 (when Vel/V=0.183), max(-Vel/V)=0.248 (when Vel/V=-0.248). So range 0.431. So ( -Vel/V + 0.183 ) / 0.431.

For short pool:
- Trail5: from 31.409 to 153.819. Normalize by max (153.819).
- RSI5: mostly 100, but some lower like 94.161, 94.215, etc. So RSI5/100.
- Rng55: from 0.224 to 0.996. Use directly.
- VolPctl: from 76.587 to 97.817 (some nan), use /100.
- AutoC: from -0.633 to 0.381. -AutoC range: for AutoC=-0.633, -AutoC=0.633; for AutoC=0.381, -AutoC=-0.381. So range 1.014. Normalize: ( -AutoC + 0.381 ) / 1.014.
- ADX: from 11.863 to 71.037. (100-ADX)/100.
- Vel/V: from 0.005 to 0.628. Use /max(0.628).

Then compute RawScore as equally weighted sum of these normalized components. Then rescale to 0-100 by min-max of RawScore within each pool.

This approach is more systematic and I can compute it.

The minimum RawScore is OI with 0.3315, maximum is ICE with 0.7885. So we can scale linearly to 0-100:
score = (RawScore - min) / (max - min) * 100

min = 0.3315, max = 0.7885, range = 0.4570

Then for each ticker:
score = (RawScore - 0.3315) / 0.4570 * 100

Now for the short pool. We need similar scoring but with opposite logic.

Short pool: Winners, looking for fade. So we want:
- Large positive Trail5 (highest magnitude)
- High RSI5 (high overbought)
- High Rng55 (near top of range)
- High VolPctl (volume climax on upside)
- Negative AutoC (mean reversion)
- Low ADX (weak trend)
- Large positive Vel/V (buying climax)

We'll normalize similarly:

For short pool, define:
A' = Trail5 / max_Trail5 (max Trail5 = 153.819 from ACAS)
B' = RSI5 / 100 (but many are 100, so B'=1 for many; for others, less)
C' = Rng55 (since we want high)
D' = VolPctl / 100 (where available; for nan, we need to handle)
E' : For AutoC, we want more negative. So we define -AutoC, and normalize to 0-1. Let's find min and max of -AutoC in short pool. From data: min -AutoC = -0.381 (when AutoC=0.381), max -AutoC = 0.633 (when AutoC=-0.633). So range = 1.014. Normalized: ( -AutoC + 0.381 ) / 1.014. This gives 0 for the least negative (AutoC=0.381) and 1 for the most negative (AutoC=-0.633).
F' = (100-ADX)/100 (ADX ranges from 11.863 to 71.037, so F' from 0.2896 to 0.8814)
G' = Vel/V / max_Vel/V (max Vel/V = 0.628 from ACAS? Actually ACAS has 0.628, check: ACAS Vel/V=0.628, but also GLW has 0.533, etc. So max is 0.628.

Then RawScore_short = average of these 7 components (D' for missing VolPctl, we'll impute median or use the normalized formula with the given range. Since there are nan, we can compute the rawscore with available indicators and then adjust? Simple: for missing, we can set D' = 0.5 (median). Or we can exclude that component. I'll set D' = 0.5 for missing to be conservative.

====================================================================================================

Before Definitions: Ticker-Masked

One-date V2 score call with SEC_ identifiers, before the feature glossary was added.

640 lines, 58.1 KB

backtest_results/deepseek_reasoning_peek_anon_2009_01_06/reasoning_content.txt

CACHE: backtest_results/deepseek_reasoning_peek_anon_cache/llm_reversal_signal_v2_2009-01-06_2009-01-13_pools60_anchors3_scorepool60_mdl-deepseek-reasoner_anon.json
DATE/POOL: 2009-01-06|pool60

We are given two pools: long (60 biggest 5-day losers) and short (60 biggest 5-day winners) from Russell 1000. Need to score each ticker from 0 to 100 based on mean-reversion potential. For long pool, high score means strong snap-back setup (oversold, exhausted sellers). For short pool, high score means strong fade setup (overbought, exhausted buyers).

We have columns: MRL, MRS, Trail5, MRSig, RSI5, Rng55, Vel/V, %MA100, 52WH, Mom3m, AutoC, ADX, VolPctl.

Interpretation:
- MRL: Percent rank of 5-day loss relative to other stocks? Actually MRL is the mean reversion long score? No, MRL and MRS are given for each ticker. They sum to 100. MRL is "Mean Reversion Long" and MRS is "Mean Reversion Short"? Actually for SEC_0001, MRL=100, MRS=0. That suggests MRL is a measure of how much it's a long candidate? But we are given both pools separately. For long pool, MRL is high (near 100) for biggest losers. For short pool, MRS is high (near 100) for biggest winners. So MRL and MRS are probably pre-computed scores indicating how much the stock is in the long or short pool? But they are not used as input? They are just ranking of the 5-day move. Trail5 is the 5-day trailing return? For long pool, Trail5 is negative (e.g., -18.413). For short pool, Trail5 is positive (e.g., 153.819). MRSig is the magnitude of the move? It's absolute value of Trail5? For long, MRSig = Trail5 (positive because Trail5 is negative? Actually in long pool, MRSig = -Trail5? For SEC_0001, Trail5=-18.413, MRSig=18.413. So MRSig is the absolute move. For short, MRSig = -Trail5? Trail5=153.819, MRSig=-153.819. So MRSig is negative for winners? Actually it's signed? It's defined as magnitude but with sign? In long pool, MRSig is positive (18.413) indicating the magnitude of the loss. In short pool, MRSig is negative (-153.819) indicating the magnitude of the gain? So MRSig is the signed magnitude? Better to use absolute value.

We need to score based on exhaustion signals for mean reversion.

Key indicators for long (oversold bounce):
- RSI5: 5-day RSI. Low RSI (<30) indicates oversold. But in long pool, RSI5 values range from 0 to 51. So low RSI is good for long candidates. For example, SEC_0005 has RSI5=3.145 (very low) -> strong oversold. SEC_0024 has RSI5=0.000 -> extremely oversold.
- Rng55: 5-day range relative to 55-day range? Actually it's a ratio between 0 and 1. Low Rng55 means the stock is near the bottom of its 5-day range? For long pool, low Rng55 could indicate it's at the low end of recent range, which is bearish but also could be exhaustion? Actually for mean reversion, we want the stock to have fallen sharply but with signs that the selling is exhausted. So extremely low Rng55 (like 0.007 for SEC_0010) means the stock is at the very bottom of its 5-day range, which could mean it's oversold and might bounce. But also if it's too low, it might be in a downtrend. We need to consider combination.
- Vel/V: Velocity/Volume? Negative values for long pool. Magnitude? For long, negative Vel/V means price declining. But exhaustion might be indicated by Vel/V becoming less negative? Actually Vel/V is probably the price velocity normalized by volume? For mean reversion, we want the move to be extreme but showing signs of slowing. So if Vel/V is very negative but not as extreme as the move? Hard to interpret.

For SEC_0001:
MRSig norm: (18.413-0.169)/18.244 = 18.244/18.244=1 -> 100
RSI: (100-17.772)/51.111 = 82.228/51.111=1.609? Actually normalized RSI score: (100-RSI)/100? But we want within pool. Better to use (1 - (RSI-minRSI)/(maxRSI-minRSI)). For RSI, min=0, max=51.111. We need to pick the best fade candidates: Those with large positive move, high volume, overbought RSI, high Rng55, negative %MA100 and Mom3m (counter-trend), negative AutoC, low ADX? But ADX high might indicate trend, but if the move is against the long-term trend, high ADX could be from the downtrend? Actually ADX measures trend strength, not direction. If the stock is in a downtrend and has a rally, ADX might be moderate. For a good fade, we want the rally to be a counter-trend move in a strong downtrend? Actually mean reversion fades work best when the move is against the longer-term trend and shows exhaustion. So we want the stock to be far below its MA and 52-week high, and in a longer-term downtrend (Mom3m negative). Also, we want the rally to be on high volume (climax buying). And we want signs of exhaustion: high RSI, near top of range, and possibly negative autocorrelation.

Thus, a good short candidate would have: large positive move (big magnitude), RSI=100 (already many), high Rng55 (near top), high VolPctl, negative %MA100 (deeply below MA), negative Mom3m (strong downtrend), negative AutoC, and ADX moderate? Very high ADX might indicate strong trend (either up or down). If the rally is part of a new uptrend, ADX might be rising but still high. Since we want the rally to reverse, we want the long-term trend to still be down, so ADX might reflect the downtrend more. I'll treat lower ADX as better for reversal.

We need to score each ticker in long and short pools. The task is to output a score 0-100 for each, using the provided features.

For long pool: biggest 5-day losers, we want strongest snap-back setups. That means we want oversold, exhausted sellers, primed for upward reversion. So high MRSig (large negative return), but also signs of exhaustion: low RSI5 (very oversold), extreme reading in Rng55 (close to low? Actually Rng55 is close location in 55-day range, low values mean near 55-day low, which can indicate oversold), low Vel/V (negative and large magnitude means strong downward velocity), %MA100 negative and large (far below moving average), 52WH negative and large (far from 52-week high), Mom3m negative (downward trend), AutoC (negative autocorrelation can indicate reversal), ADX (trend strength, if high might indicate strong trend, but for mean reversion we might want high ADX to confirm the move? Actually high ADX indicates strong trend, which could mean the move is not exhausted yet, but for mean reversion we want extreme move with signs of exhaustion, so perhaps moderate ADX? But let's think: In mean reversion, we want a sharp move that is overdone. High ADX could mean the trend is strong and may continue, so not good for reversion. Low ADX could mean trend is weak and it's just noise. So probably we want high ADX to confirm the move was significant, but also signs of exhaustion. Hmm. Actually typical mean reversion strategies look for extreme moves in strong trends that are showing reversal signals. But the key is exhaustion: volume climax, etc. But we don't have volume. We have AutoC: negative autocorrelation at lag 5 can indicate the recent moves are reversing themselves, so that's a positive for reversion. Also VolPctl: high volatility regime might mean moves are more likely to revert? Or maybe lower volatility is better? Usually mean reversion works better in higher volatility environments because moves are more extreme. So high VolPctl could be good.

For short pool: biggest 5-day winners, we want fade setups. So high RSI5 (overbought), high Rng55 (near 55-day high), high Vel/V (positive and large), high %MA100 (above MA), 52WH near high, Mom3m positive, AutoC negative (again, reversal signal?), ADX moderate to high, VolPctl high.

But we need to score each individually, not just rank. The score should use the full 0-100 range. We can create a composite score by normalizing and combining relevant features. Since we have many features, we need a systematic approach.

Let's think about which features are most indicative for mean reversion.

For long pool (oversold bounce):
- MRSig: the recent 5-day loss, more negative is better. But since all are losers, this varies. Actually Trail5 is negative, MRSig is positive. Higher MRSig means larger loss. So we want high MRSig.
- RSI5: lower means more oversold. So lower RSI5 better.
- Rng55: lower means near 55-day low, which can indicate oversold condition. So lower is better.
- Vel/V: negative, more negative means stronger downward momentum. But we want exhaustion, so maybe extreme negative then we expect reversal. So more negative (lower) might be better.
- %MA100: negative, more negative means further below MA, oversold. So lower is better.
- 52WH: negative, more negative means far from 52-week high, oversold. So lower better.
- Mom3m: negative, more negative means strong downtrend. But for mean reversion, we want a sharp move in a downtrend? Actually if the stock is in a strong downtrend, a snap-back might be less likely because the trend is down. So might prefer less negative Mom3m? But the biggest losers might already be in downtrend. We need to think: mean reversion often works best when the stock is not in a persistent trend, but just a sharp move. So perhaps Mom3m closer to zero or positive? But in this pool, many have negative Mom3m. We could use it as a contrarian indicator: if Mom3m is very negative, the stock might be in a deep downtrend and the bounce may be short-lived. So for snap-back, we might want the move to be a sharp correction within a larger uptrend or sideways. So we might want Mom3m not too negative. But the biggest losers already have negative trend. So we could penalize extremely negative Mom3m.
- AutoC: negative AutoC suggests that the recent returns are negatively correlated with returns 5 days ago, which could indicate reversals. So negative AutoC is good for reversion. The more negative, the better.
- ADX: trend strength. For mean reversion, we might want high ADX to confirm the move was significant, but if ADX is too high, the trend might continue. So maybe moderate? But let's see: In the long pool, ADX ranges from 10 to 53. We can use it as a signal: best when ADX is high enough to indicate a strong move, but not extremely high to suggest trend persistence? Actually many mean reversion strategies use ADX to confirm the move is not just noise. But we don't have clear rule. Let's think about the typical mean reversion candidate: a stock that had a sharp, high-volume (but no volume here) move that is overdone. ADX > 25 is typically considered a strong trend. For a mean reversion, we want the move to be part of a trend that is exhausted, so ADX might be high but not extremely high. But we have to decide.
- VolPctl: high volatility regime might mean the stock is more likely to revert because it's in a high-vol environment. So high VolPctl better.

For long pool:

- Higher MRSig (or Trail5 magnitude)

So for long pool, we want high MRSig positive. If MRSig is negative, that's actually a gain, so not a loser. So we want to penalize that.

Let's create a scoring model.

I'll do a simple approach: for each pool, standardize each relevant feature (z-score) and then combine with equal weights (or some weights). But we need to handle extreme values and ensure scores are 0-100.

Alternative: rank each feature within the pool and sum ranks, then normalize to 0-100. That is robust.

Given we have 60 tickers per pool, we can rank them on each indicator (with appropriate direction) and then average the percentile ranks.

Let's define the direction for each feature.

For LONG POOL:
- MRSig: higher is better (more negative return, but MRSig is positive for losers). So rank descending.
- RSI5: lower is better (oversold). So rank ascending (lowest rank = best).
- Rng55: lower is better (near 55-day low). So rank ascending.
- Vel/V: lower (more negative) is better. So rank ascending.
- %MA100: lower (more negative) is better. So rank ascending.
- 52WH: lower (more negative) is better. So rank ascending.
- Mom3m: higher is better (less negative or positive). So rank descending.
- AutoC: lower (more negative) is better. So rank ascending.
- ADX: we need to decide. Looking at ENDPQ, high ADX. Also at other strong candidates? Let's check CELG: MRL 98.86, RSI5 39.98, Rng55 0.239, Vel/V -0.124, %MA100 -15.76, 52WH -34.8, Mom3m -9.76, AutoC -0.08, ADX 13.292, VolPctl 84.3. Not as extreme. So ENDPQ has much higher ADX. So high ADX seems associated with strong move. For mean reversion, we want the move to be significant, so high ADX might be good. But is there a risk that high ADX indicates trend continuation? In the case of ENDPQ, it's a -18% drop in 5 days, ADX 53.5, which is very high. That could mean the trend is strong and may continue. But we are looking for a snap-back, so maybe it's too trended? I think it's still a good candidate because the drop is so extreme and RSI is very low. So I'll assume higher ADX is better for long pool (confirms the move is not noise). So rank descending.
- VolPctl: higher is better (high vol regime). So rank descending.

For SHORT POOL:
- MRSig? Actually for short pool, the recent move is positive, but MRSig is negative (since it's negative of Trail5). So we want more negative MRSig (larger gain) for stronger fade. So lower MRSig is better. So rank ascending.
- RSI5: higher is better (overbought). So rank descending.
- Rng55: higher is better (near 55-day high). So rank descending.
- Vel/V: higher (more positive) is better. So rank descending.
- %MA100: higher (closer to zero or positive) is better. So rank descending.
- 52WH: higher (less negative) is better. So rank descending.
- Mom3m: higher (less negative) is better? Actually for short pool, many have negative Mom3m because they are coming from deep lows. For a fade, we want the bounce to be overdone, so if the stock is still in a deep downtrend, the bounce might be more likely to reverse. So more negative Mom3m might be better? Actually if the stock has been in a strong downtrend, a sharp bounce is more likely to be a dead cat bounce, so good for fade. So we want lower Mom3m (more negative) for short pool. So rank ascending.
- AutoC: lower (more negative) is better (indicates reversal from up to down). So rank ascending.
- ADX: we want high ADX to confirm the move was strong? But for fade, high ADX might indicate the bounce is strong and might continue. However, if ADX is very high, it might be a strong trend, not a reversal. I think moderate ADX might be better for fade. But let's check some top names: ACAS has ADX 29.8, SITC 29.66, KWKAQ 32.8, CCO 37.5. Not extremely high. Some have very high like CBLAQ 55.8, PL 71.0. That might be too high and indicate the trend is very strong, so less likely to reverse? But we need to decide. I'll lean towards higher ADX being good for fade as it confirms the move is significant, but with caution. Let's look at the data: PL has ADX 71, which is extremely high, and RSI 100, Rng55 0.996, Vel/V 0.299. That could be a strong reversal candidate. I think high ADX could be good because it shows the move is not just noise and the exhaustion might lead to reversal. So I'll rank descending for short pool as well.
- VolPctl: higher is better (high vol regime). So rank descending.

Now, we have 10 features for each pool. We'll compute the percentile rank (0 to 1) for each ticker on each feature within its pool, then average the ranks. Then multiply by 100 to get score from 0 to 100. This ensures full range.

But note: For some features, there may be missing values (like SDOCQ VolPctl nan, CPN 52WH nan, etc.). We need to handle NaNs. In such cases, we can exclude that feature from the averaging for that ticker, or impute with the mean rank. Since we are ranking within the pool, we can rank non-NaN values and then assign NaN the mean rank (0.5). That seems reasonable.

Also, for the long pool, we have one ticker (ESINQ) with MRSig 2.57 but Trail5 -2.57? Actually ESINQ has MRSig 2.57, so it's a loser. All long pool tickers have positive MRSig except USB and NST which have negative MRSig (they are actually gainers). So for those, their rank on MRSig will be very low. That's fine.

- Top: ENDPQ, ICE, RAI, WRB, (maybe CVSA, CFR) -> scores 90-100
- Middle: many others

For short pool:
- Top: ACAS, CCO, OSK, LNC, BEAV, PL, etc. -> scores 90-100
- Bottom: those with smaller moves or poor exhaustion signals.

But we need exact scores for each ticker. I'll try to create a systematic ranking.

Given the time, I will generate scores by a simple formula in my mind: for each ticker, I will consider the following components and give a score between 0 and 100:

For long score:
- Score_MRSig = (MRSig / max(MRSig)) * 100
- Score_RSI = (1 - RSI5/100) * 100
- Score_Rng55 = (1 - Rng55) * 100
- Score_VelV = ( -Vel/V ) normalized? Actually Vel/V is negative, let's use ( -Vel/V / max_abs(Vel/V) ) * 100
- Score_MA100 = ( -%MA100 / 100 ) but %MA100 can be positive, so better to use ( -%MA100 ) if negative, else 0.
- Score_52WH = ( -52WH / 100 ) similarly.
- Score_Mom3m = (Mom3m, but positive is better, so normalize with max positive)
- Score_AutoC = ( -AutoC ) if negative, else 0.
- Score_ADX = (ADX / 100) * 100
- Score_Vol = (VolPctl / 100) * 100

Then average these standardized scores. But this is crude.

To make it easier, I'll compute a composite ranking by sorting the tickers based on a combination of key features: For long: MRSig, RSI5 (low), Rng55 (low), AutoC (negative), ADX (high), VolPctl (high). I'll assign weights.

Let's suppose weights: MRSig 0.2, RSI5 0.2, Rng55 0.15, AutoC 0.1, ADX 0.15, VolPctl 0.1, others 0.1. I'll estimate each ticker's score.
